Basecamp's ONCE Campfire · the F# port, unchained

onCrack.

The same Campfire, pixel for pixel, on a server that is free to do less work: it keeps finished pages until a commit changes what they show, and commits concurrent posts together. Measured with Basecamp's own verification harness, in one session, beside current upstream Rails, Rust and C and the F# port it was forked from. Every number on this page links to the file it came from.

The honest version · round one, pinned Rust ccece30

Cache hits, and the other end.

A stored page is only as good as the writes that don't touch it. So the same room page was read at 16 connections while one more connection posted into that very room as fast as each app would take it: about 1,100 posts a second, each one invalidating the page.

Room page reads a second at 16 connections, same scale in both. How it was measured →

What onCrack does that Rust and the port don't

Less work per request.

The HTML is byte for byte the port's and Rust's. What changed is how often it gets made, and how it goes out. Each idea states the rule that keeps it correct.

Versioned read cache

A trigger bumps a counter inside the write's own transaction. A stored page is served only while every counter it was built under is unchanged.

Group commit

Posts that arrive together share one transaction, a savepoint each, one commit. Every post is committed before it is answered.

One send, not two

A cached gzip page goes out with its Content-Length instead of chunked, so it no longer needs a second send to end it.

Native AOT, opt-in

Starts in 98 ms instead of 218, but serves less at 16 connections, so the default stays JIT.

The four, with their rules and their numbers →

Where Rust still leads

One message, a thousand clients.

A post goes out over Action Cable to everyone in the room. onCrack hasn't touched that code yet: it is the port's, and it trails Rust, from 0.87× at 100 clients to 0.61× at 1,000. That is the next round.

Where it came from

Start with the port.

onCrack is a fork. The F# port is the faithful one: the same work per request as the Rust port, translated module by module, and still 1.33× Rust at 16 connections in this session. If you want to read how Campfire works in F#, read that.

onCrack is a fork of the F# port of Basecamp's ONCE Campfire. Campfire, the Rust port, its parity harness and vectors are © 37signals, LLC, MIT. Generated from the results by site/build.py, which refuses to show a number it cannot source.

Same pixels, four engines

How we know

×

All Talk, the busy room, signed in as David: the page the room_show benchmark requests. Each picture is the parity harness's own capture of that page from one app: Chromium, 1,440×900, light scheme, the default seed with the clock frozen.

Same pixels, checked. The four PNGs are byte-identical (SHA-256 ac7420e6d0a89414…) and decode to the same pixels as the port's check recorded (3c57af02305d5b10…). The thumbnails are made one from each app's own capture and come out the same bytes, which site/build.py checks on every build before it shows the badge. The big screenshot is onCrack's own capture, lossless. In round one's gate this cell matched Rails on every layer the harness compares: pixels, server, live, aria, network, cable.

Source: bench/results/oncrack-round1-final/review/pixel-parity.json, site/same_pixels.py, site/data.json.

Reads while writing

The honest read/write mix

×

bench/run's read workloads ask for one URL over and over and never write, so onCrack answers every request from a stored page. The table on the main page (4.34× Rust on the room page) is that best case. This is the other end.

How

review/mixed.sh: 16 connections read the room page while one connection posts into the same room as fast as the app answers. Every post moves the room's counter, so every post throws the stored page away. 30 s together as a warm-up, then a measured window; two runs per app, in opposite orders (6 runs in all).

Reads and posts a second: mean of the two runs (latency: the first run)
Reads/s, c=16Posts/s, c=1Read p50 / p99 msCPU µs/req
Rust18,6196780.82 / 1.78178.5
F# port24,3727800.67 / 1.59147.9
onCrack42,7521,1070.19 / 1.1185.2
onCrack / Rust2.30×1.63×0.48×
onCrack / port1.75×1.42×0.58×
  • Still mostly hits. onCrack serves about 39 reads for every change to the room, so this is a mix of stored pages and fresh renders, not the miss path alone.
  • The miss path alone is the port's. With CAMPFIRE_READ_CACHE=off the room page reads 39,379 a second against 95,310 on (tier 2, one rep, c=16; the cache's numbers), which is the port's speed: the cache adds no cost when it misses.
  • Correct while it raced. Every response in every run was a 200, and the rows written equal the posts answered (checked from the file on every build).

Source: bench/results/oncrack-round1-final/review/mixed.jsonl, bench/results/oncrack-round1-final/review/mixed.sh, bench/results/oncrack-round1-final.md.

What onCrack does that Rust and the port don't

Four changes, four rules

×

Four changes, measured one at a time against the build without them. The HTML a browser gets is unchanged (its decoded bytes equal Rust's on every probed route), and each change has a switch that turns it off.

The versioned read cache

A global counter, one per room, one per user and one for sessions. Triggers bump them inside the write's transaction. Room counters are striped: ids equal modulo 65,536 share one, so a URL naming rooms that don't exist can't grow memory (the verifier's finding).

The triggers (TEMP, on the writer's connection only, so the database file keeps Rails' schema)
A write toBumps
messagesits room
boosts; a message's rich text and attachmentsthe message's room (global if the message is gone)
roomsthe room; created or destroyed: the room and global
membershipsadded or removed: the room and the user; updated: the user
searches, push subscriptionsthe user
sessionssessions
accounts, users, bans, webhooks, blobs, variants, other rich textglobal
the search index (no triggers possible)the message's room, by the job that writes it
What each stored page depends on
PageDepends on
room page, messages pagethe global counter (under which its user was read), the user, the room
sidebar, searchthe global counter, the user, every room the user is in
the session (authentication)the sessions counter
the signed-in userthe global counter

The invalidation rule. The first bump of a counter in a write makes it odd, before the row changes; the write's end (after the commit and its after-commit work, before the caller hears back) makes it even, one past. A request takes its counters before its first query, keeps the page only if all were even and are unchanged when it finishes, and a stored page is served only while every counter still equals what it was kept under. So nothing is served across a committed change, and a writer always reads its own write.

Never shared across users: the key starts with the user's id, then everything the render reads from the request (route, full URL, host, Turbo-Frame, Referer, User-Agent, the last_room cookie). A request with a flash message is not cached. The store is bounded at 64 MB in two generations. CAMPFIRE_READ_CACHE=off turns it off.

Tier 2: the same build with the cache off and on, one rep each
Workload, c=16Cache offCache onOn / offCPU µs/req
room page39,37995,3102.42×90.6 → 32.2
messages page42,134103,2682.45×84.8 → 30.7
sidebar38,162110,5232.90×94.6 → 27.3
search33,72971,4722.12×108.3 → 31.4
post a message8,6268,7351.01×364.0 → 354.5

Sources: bench/results/oncrack-c1.md, bench/results/oncrack-c1/t2-cache-off/oncrack-1.json, bench/results/oncrack-c1/t2-cache-on/oncrack-1.json, bench/results/oncrack-fixes.md.

Group commit

The single writer takes every write already queued (up to 64) and runs them in one transaction, a savepoint each, with one COMMIT; then the group's after-commit work in one more transaction; then the cache counters are released; then every caller is answered, in queue order. It never waits for a group to fill (the window is 0 µs), so at one connection every group is one write.

The durability rule. Every post is committed before its response is written: WAL with synchronous=normal, Rails' own setting. A write that fails rolls back to its savepoint alone; anything that breaks the group's transaction leaves nothing committed and the group runs again write by write. Broadcasts go out in commit order.

Tier 2, posting: one write per transaction against grouped, one rep each
ConnectionsGroup commit offOnOn / offCPU µs/req
12,9172,9841.02×655.2 → 639.2
168,63111,9471.38×361.7 → 256.6
646,10314,6922.41×347.7 → 215.1

Sources: bench/results/oncrack-c2.md, bench/results/oncrack-c2/t2-group-off/oncrack-1.json, bench/results/oncrack-c2/t2-group-on/oncrack-1.json.

Content-Length on cached gzip answers

The port copies Rack::Deflater and sends gzip chunked, which costs a second send for the terminating chunk. For a gzipped buffer or a stored page onCrack makes the gzip member before the headers, sends its length and writes it once. Streams, files and pages rendered in parts stay chunked. CAMPFIRE_GZIP_LENGTH=off restores chunking. On a search at c=16, syscalls per request fell from 18.1 to 10.0.

Tier 2: the same build with the setting off and on, one rep each
Workloadc=16 chunkedc=16 lengthc=16 gainc=1 gain
room page100,974122,4771.21×1.12×
messages page102,770122,3581.19×1.13×
sidebar115,253137,1231.19×1.21×
search72,058136,8051.90×1.10×
post a message11,81913,0491.10×1.00×

Sources: bench/results/oncrack-c3.md, bench/results/oncrack-c3/t2-length-off/oncrack-1.json, bench/results/oncrack-c3/t2-length-on/oncrack-1.json.

Native AOT, as an opt-in

docker build --build-arg NATIVE_AOT=true makes campfire-oncrack:aot. It starts in 98 ms against the default image's 218, idles at 22 MB against 38, and passes the pixel gate (361 screenshots, the fast matrix on the default seed). But without tiered PGO it serves 0.90× of the default image at 16 connections (1.14× at one), and it loses HTTP/2 without TLS. The headline is throughput at 16 connections, so the default stays JIT + ReadyToRun.

Sources: bench/results/oncrack-c4.md, bench/results/oncrack-c4/boot.jsonl, bench/results/oncrack-c4/tier3-aot/oncrack-1.json, bench/results/oncrack-c3/tier3/oncrack-1.json.

Correctness evidence

Every attack, and what it found

×

Before the round was scored, a separate reviewer was told to break units C1-C3: stale pages, one user's data in another's page, lost or reordered posts, unbounded memory. It wrote 13 attack tests: 7 held, and 6 found problems, which came down to 1 major bug and 3 minor ones. Every finding was fixed with a test that fails without the fix.

Every attack test the verifier ran
TestWhat it attacksHowResultLog
A1read cache: a rename of the signed-in user, room page1,500 writes; after each returns, a fresh request is checked while 12 other browsers read the same pagefound a major bugstale 93, 75; checks 1,500a1-cache-on.log a1-cache-off.log
A1bread cache: a rename of the signed-in user, sidebar1,500 writes under 12 concurrent readersfound a major bugstale 50; checks 1,500a1-cache-on.log a1-cache-off.log

The major bug: a stale user row

What: authentication read the signed-in user before the page took its counters. A change to that user committing in between was rendered from the old row and stored under the new counters, then served until an unrelated write moved one: the name in the room page's meta tag and composer, the sidebar's name and avatar, and the administrator-only New Chat Room button.

Found by A1, A1b and A1c, each making 1,500 changes while 12 other browsers read the page: 93 and 75 stale room pages in two runs, 50 stale sidebars, 79 sidebars showing the wrong role. With the cache off, all three found 0.

The fix and its rule: a page's global dependency is now the global counter's value the user was read under, not one taken when the action starts. A page whose user changed since is neither kept nor served; a page whose user was not read through the cache is rendered but not kept. Three tests in ReadCacheTests make 1,000 changes while 12 browsers read; before the fix each failed, after it all pass twice in a row, and also with the cache off.

The minor ones

  • A counter for every room id in a URL (A2): fixed by striping room counters modulo 65,536, at most a 512 KB array per database.
  • Group commit broadcast concurrent posts out of commit order (A5), more often than the port: fixed with a line of places taken in commit order; now all 320 posts of the test arrive with 0 out-of-order pairs, group commit on and off.
  • bin/kit-differential http failed by default because of the added Content-Length: it now accepts exactly the gzip member's length and nothing else, with a self-test.

Gates

Before the fixes the verifier also ran bin/verify, bin/db-differential, bin/kit-differential and the full pixel gate; after them bin/verify ran 1,075 tests with 0 failed. The verifier's report is kept verbatim beside its tests.

Sources: bench/results/oncrack-verifier/verifier.json, bench/results/oncrack-verifier/AttackTests.fs, bench/results/oncrack-verifier/killtest.py, bench/results/oncrack-verifier/logs/session-results.log, bench/results/oncrack-fixes.md.

Durability

Rails' default, and nothing weaker

×

onCrack ships Rails' durability and nothing weaker. The plan allowed weaker settings only behind a switch whose default is Rails'; none was needed. Group commit shares the commit, not the durability: Rails' synchronous=normal already doesn't fsync per commit, so there was no per-post sync to share.

Default against the alternatives
SettingDefaultWhat it doesThe other way
CAMPFIRE_GROUP_COMMITonGrouped writes, a savepoint each, one commit. Every post commits before it is answered.off: one write per transaction, the port's writer
CAMPFIRE_GROUP_COMMIT_WINDOW_US0The writer never waits for a group to fill.a wait only adds latency; durability is the same
journal_mode, synchronouswal, normalRails' own settings, unchanged.nothing weaker exists to turn on
synchronous=fullnot builtStronger than Rails: an fsync per commit, which group commit could share.not built or measured

Killed mid-write

The verifier posted from 16 threads with the default settings, killed the container with SIGKILL mid-run, restarted it on the same storage and looked for every post that had been answered 200. 3 runs: 9,656 acknowledged posts, 0 lost, 0 duplicated, 0 missing from the search index, and the newest acknowledged post was on the room page after the restart. A few posts per run were stored but never acknowledged: committed, killed before the answer went out, which is the order the rule promises.

Measured both ways

Posts a second, tier 2, one rep each, same build
ConnectionsGroup commit offOn (the default)On, window of 200 µs (not kept)
12,9172,9841,870
168,63111,94710,946
646,10314,69216,172

A commit window costs the single connection its whole wait per post and gains nothing at 16 or 64 connections, where the queue already fills groups, so it stays at 0.

Sources: bench/results/oncrack-verifier/verifier.json, bench/results/oncrack-verifier/killtest.py, bench/results/oncrack-c2.md, bench/results/oncrack-c2/t2-group-off/oncrack-1.json, bench/results/oncrack-c2/t2-group-on/oncrack-1.json, bench/results/oncrack-c2/t2-window200/oncrack-1.json.

Pixel parity

Every screenshot, per seed

×

The rule onCrack works under: every parity screen's pixels must match Rails'. The server's HTML, the live DOM and Cable frames may differ; what a person sees may not.

Round one's gate, per seed
SeedEqualScreenshotsDifferentNot capturedFlakyBy engine
crowd2424000chromium 24
custom_styles3232000chromium 32
default860860000chromium 788, firefox 36, webkit 36
first_run1616000chromium 16
restricted88000chromium 8
all940940000

How it was run

  • bin/pixel-parity against the Rails reference, the harness's lean matrix: every state on Chromium (desktop and phone, light and dark), the smoke states on Firefox and WebKit, and the breakpoint sweep, on all 5 seeds. Image campfire-candidate-oncrack built from da1e92f, the image that was benchmarked.
  • The default seed ran in ten --only slices so each fit one call; together they cover the same cells as a full run. A cell that could not be captured (a stylesheet wait that ran out at 30,000 ms on freshly started servers, older than onCrack) was captured again and counts by its last attempt, as bin/pixel-parity counts it.
  • Before the fixes the verifier ran the gate in one piece on the pre-fix image: also all equal, in 3,843 s. The Native AOT image passed the fast matrix on the default seed: 361 screenshots.

The reports themselves (every screenshot and diff) are gitignored under parity/out/pixel-parity-round1-final/; pixel_parity.py keeps what they say.

Sources: bench/results/oncrack-round1-final/review/pixel-parity.json, bench/results/oncrack-round1-final/review/pixel_parity.py, bin/pixel-parity, bin/pixel-parity-report.

← Back to the page

Measurement ceiling

Is the load generator the limit?

×

A fast server can be under-measured by a slow client. The load generator runs on its own 4 cores, and the session recorded how busy they were in every window.

onCrack's workloads at 64 connections, and the fastest cell of the whole session
CellcReq/sServer cores busyLoad generator cores busyLoad generator µs/req
onCrack, room page64128,3953.763.1024.2
onCrack, messages page64135,8723.722.8621.0
onCrack, sidebar64154,8833.763.0419.6
onCrack, search64152,2333.783.0420.0
onCrack, post a message6417,6843.310.5430.7
onCrack, CSS file64217,9483.733.1514.5
  • At onCrack's fastest workload cell the load generator used 3.04 of its 4 cores, leaving 1.0 idle, while the server used 3.76 of its 4: the server is the one at its limit.
  • The rig went faster in the same session: the same load generator drove 217,948 requests a second at the CSS file (onCrack, 64 connections), 1.41× onCrack's fastest workload cell. So the workload numbers are not the rig's ceiling.
  • The host stayed quiet: after each of the 288 measured windows a probe timed a fixed spin on the pinned CPUs; the worst was 1.08× its baseline.
  • Not measured: a second load generator, or a client on another machine. The VM has 8 CPUs, split evenly, so neither side can be given more without taking from the other.

Counted twice

A hand run outside bench/run (review/manual.sh) counted requests three ways: the load generator, the server's own request log, and rows in the database.

Fixed warm-up, then a window; onCrack's log lines trail by its batched log's last flush

How the picture is drawn

Each lane sends pulses from its hub at a rate in proportion to its measured messages a second; at a given N one pulse stands for the same number of messages in every lane, and each dot for ten clients. The pulses are CSS animations, so they run without JavaScript; with JavaScript each lane also counts what it has delivered since you opened the page, and how far behind Rust that leaves it. With reduced motion nothing moves and the rates are what you read.

Sources (and the other reps beside each): bench/results/oncrack-round1-final/oncrack-1.json, bench/results/oncrack-round1-final/rust-1.json, bench/results/oncrack-round1-final/fsharp-1.json, bench/results/oncrack-round1-final/reference-1.json, bench/loadgen/src/main.rs.

Reproduce it

The commands

×

Everything runs inside the colima VM (colima ssh -- bash -c "cd $PWD && ..."), from the repository root, on branch crack. Each app's rep runs in parts, one container per part, merged into the rep's file, so every part fits one call. Order: Rails, Rust, the port, onCrack in reps one and three, reversed in rep two.

CAMPFIRE_REVISION=$(git rev-parse HEAD) parity/bin/candidate build     # then bin/clean-docker from macOS
R=bench/results/oncrack-round1-final/review
$R/quiet.sh && $R/part.sh reference 1 'room_show messages_page'   # ... sidebar search | avatar static_css up | post_message
$R/quiet.sh && $R/part.sh oncrack 1 'post_message' 'http cable upload'   # each rep's last part
bench/report bench/results/oncrack-round1-final > bench/results/oncrack-round1-final/report.md
python3 bench/results/oncrack-round1-final/review/table.py
$R/manual.sh rust; $R/manual.sh oncrack; $R/mixed.sh rust|fsharp|oncrack; $R/bytes.sh
bin/pixel-parity --seeds crowd,custom_styles,first_run,restricted
bin/pixel-parity --seeds default --only 'realtime/**'   # ... ten slices
python3 bench/results/oncrack-round1-final/review/pixel_parity.py
python3 site/same_pixels.py && python3 site/build.py

Fixed

  • Seed: parity/.seed/default, copied fresh for every run; signed in as David.
  • CPU pinning: the server on CPUs 0-3, the load generator on 4-7, host networking (SERVER_CPUS, LOADGEN_CPUS).
  • Schedule: apps reference,rust,fsharp,oncrack, reps 3; suites: http; http secs 8, concs 1 16 64, routes room_show messages_page, warm-up 30s at each route and concentration, idle 10s.
  • Warm-up: every route at its measured concurrency until flat (bench/lib/warmup.py); the VM's load waited down before every part (review/quiet.sh).
  • Durability: the defaults. No setting that weakens it was used.

Read with care

  • Not their machine. Four of this VM's CPUs per side, not the Ryzen's; compare ratios, not absolute numbers, with their report.
  • A different freshness mechanism. Upstream re-reads the session and membership on every cache hit. onCrack keeps the decision and drops it on any commit, by any connection (it watches SQLite's WAL index); the round's verifier attacked that with foreign writers and it held. The cost of a hit is not the same work as theirs.
  • Search order. onCrack and the port render Rails 90b3300, whose search lists matches by time; upstream now lists them by id. Their search is validated against the old order.
  • One viewer, one URL. Their read workloads are a cache-hit benchmark; the appendix of the results file keeps this project's realistic workloads.

The climb

How to read the chart

×

The line is the geometric mean of onCrack's throughput over Rust's across the five benchmarked workloads (room page, messages page, sidebar, search, post a message) at 16 connections. Dots are spaced evenly in the order they ran. A dot that measured only some workloads carries the rest from the last kept run; its card greys them.

Read with care

  • The denominator is stored. Every dot divides by the pinned Rust port's (ccece30) medians from phase7-final, one earlier session, so the dots compare with each other. Round one measured that Rust beside onCrack in one session and the two agree closely (the five workloads). The headline at the top of the page is current upstream, measured with upstream's harness.
  • The first dot is not onCrack. It is the F# port's final result, the starting point the fork is scored from.
  • Most dots are provisional. A tier 2 dot is one rep of the workloads a change touches, on a build mounted into a container. The numbers that count are tier 3: a rebuilt image, three reps, all five workloads.
  • Rails is in the cards only. At a few percent of Rust's throughput it would sit flat on the floor of the chart.

← Back to the page

The rules

What onCrack may and may not do

×

From plans/oncrack.md: “Make the UI exactly the same, but go unhinged with Datastar, caching, or any other improvements we can do.” The port matched the Rust port's work per request and still beat it. onCrack drops that constraint, and nothing else.

  1. The UI is identical. Every parity screen's pixels match Rails' (the harness's screenshot layer, lean matrix, all seeds) and every interaction behaves the same for a person using it. Server HTML, the live DOM, network requests and Cable frames may differ.
  2. Correct, always. No stale reads after a write, no data from one user in another's response, no lost messages. Every cache states its invalidation rule and has tests that try to break it.
  3. Durability is a disclosed choice. Anything that weakens it is measured both ways, documented, and shipped only behind a setting whose default is Rails-equivalent durability.
  4. Scored separately. Same harness, same seed and pinned CPUs; every ratio against the Rust port and against the F# port, never presented as a like-for-like port result.
  5. Same discipline. Measure before and after, one change at a time, log every run to the climb chart, never manufacture a win.

Source: plans/oncrack.md.

← Back to the page

Before onCrack

The F# port's climb

×

onCrack was forked from the F# port, a faithful translation of the Rust port that matched Rust's work per request. The port's Phase 7 tuning climbed from 0.76× to 1.32× of Rust; these dots are kept off onCrack's line because they measured a different rule (the same work as Rust) against a stored Rust baseline from an earlier session. The port itself is at github.com/ckluis/once-campfire-fSharp.

Before the port

Claimed F#/Rust ratios from the challenge app. They are off the chart on purpose: the pages weren't identical to Rust's, so they don't measure the same work.

Muse, Ren's F# app (Linux box)

F# wins all 5

  • Room page3.1×
  • Messages page7.3×
  • Sidebar5.9×
  • Search9.3×
  • Post a message1.31×

Not comparable: F# served lighter pages than the Rust port, the messages workload hit a 404/204 in the official app, and the first post number came from unauthenticated redirects.

Same app, rebaselined on the M4 (5 Oct)

Harness bugs fixed

  • Room page1.83×
  • Messages page1.93×
  • Sidebar5.4×
  • Search28×
  • Post a message1.74×

Still not comparable: 50 vs 40 messages a page, a hardcoded timestamp, reversed search order and 30-60% smaller pages. So the work became a full, identical-in-use port, and every dot below renders the same bytes as Rust.
